```
To be clear, much of what I said has been said by the leaders of other labs. What I *am* saying is this path is not inevitable.

I am saying leaders and leadership matter more than ever, and that’s why I’m proud of the work that Mark and I have done at Meta to put safety as a top focus of our efforts, including delaying the launch of Muse for months and being a leader on the SI Accords that all of our peers have signed onto. We are investing more in safety every day, and have implemented rigorous processes to make sure every step we take is safe and positive. Mark has also called for all labs (including our own) to deploy the vast majority of compute towards serving people and customers rather than towards recursive self-improvement (RSI).
```

## 2119677603862692029

- time: 2026-11-09 14:24:57 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2119677603862692029
- type: original
- lang: en

```
i forgot about this until @ScottWu46 reminded me, but back in january 2017 when we were 19, scott and i had lunch in SF

at lunch, we talked about how our IMO/IOI/math competition gang could be the next paypal mafia. i knew the competition kids were geniuses by any standard, and felt confident in time we could take the tech world by storm

fast forward nearly 10 years later, and many of our friends from that time are some of the most exciting people in AI and technology (@ScottWu46, @akshat_b, @chameleon_jeff, Jerry Ma, @randomjohnnyh, @0xShitTrader, @stevenkplus1, @thejessezhang, @vickie_ye_, @alexwei_, @demi_guo_, @d_m_ziegler, @hughbzhang, @_mohansolo, @MarkSellke, and many others i'm sure i'm forgetting)

i'm now at that age where i reminisce about everyone i grew up with, and i've known most of these people since middle school (via MATHCOUNTS!). even though we were scattered around the country, we would gchat about math problems and hang out on AoPS, then we did math/algo camps together, and then we did internships together.

very crazy to think about the degree of sheer brilliance of this group when we were just teenagers, and so special to see so many go on to leave their mark on the world.

note to parents: if your kid is on gchat all day solving math problems with some anonymous group of cross-country asian kids, (s)he may just turn out alright.
```

## 2117181858538791347

- time: 2026-11-02 17:07:45 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2117181858538791347
- type: original
- lang: en

```
wildwood is a true marvel of human artistry, craftsmanship, and storytelling. i honestly found it even better than You Can See Everything (gasp!).

[spoilers if you read on]

an interesting trend in both wildwood and hoppers is how they handle death. they’re both movies for young people that treat death as inevitable and natural, and both expose the audience to a surprising amount of it. this feels quite different from the cleaner moral universes of much of modern disney and pixar.

both movies also have strong themes of non-intervention. of course, this is environmentally motivated, but there’s a broader Buddhist / Taoist morality running through both films: the world has a natural balance, and our attempts to impose our own ideas of goodness upon it can be destructive.

this feels quite different from the more Christian-inflected morality of overcoming evil. in these films, nature isn’t inherently good or evil, and trying to eliminate everything we perceive as bad can itself be the source of harm.

i wonder if this reflects a broader generational shift toward stories where it’s increasingly difficult to know what’s “right” or “wrong”, and where good intentions aren’t enough to make an action good.

both films feel like spiritual siblings to princess mononoke, questioning the hubris of humans who believe they can impose their will on the natural world.

wildwood is also visually spectacular and a complete and utter artistic achievement. it is always so rewarding to see fresh new takes on what a movie can be and how stories can be told.

huge fan, go see it!
```

- type: original
- lang: en

```
REMY OR LINGUINI?

for those of you who have seen ratatouille, one of the great questions in the ai age: are we remy or linguini?

are the humans in control of the combined entity (we are remy), or are the ai in control and we are simply the meat proxies (we are linguini)?

our vision with muse is undeniably the former—we are remy! we have big dreams and goals, many of which may not be appropriate for our station or our exact starting condition. but that doesn't matter—we dream like a rat who wants to be a michelin-starred chef in paris.

muse is our partner to help make that happen. ultimately, we are pulling the hairs (?), and it is our vision that ends up on the plate (?), but over time we will accomplish things that we could not do without muse.

(obviously this metaphor is super confusing because i'm saying the humans are the rat, but if you watched the movie you catch my drift)

anyway this whole mini-essay was just to justify my shitpost—pls enjoy
```

```
i have been using muse wonder full to up my shitpost game
```

## 2108456337374065097

- time: 2026-10-09 15:15:38 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2108456337374065097
- type: quote
- lang: en

```
we launched muse a month ago today

for the past month, reality felt like a dream. we watched the world catch fire with muse. every day we read stories of people saving money and precious time. every day it made people’s lives better in small and big ways. every day i watched the muse team pour their hearts into bringing a little more wonder in the world.

the response to muse has surpassed anything i could have imagined. i know the muse team will remember it for the rest of our lives.

it is easy to become jaded in tech. products launch every day but they very rarely make any dent in the world. but i have seen muse make a real difference for moms, for grandpas, for students, for coworkers. many of you using it in ways we never expected. 

thank you to the muse team. thank you to everyone who’s tried muse. this is only the beginning. we have so much more to build.
```

## 2100717269861167524

- time: 2026-09-18 06:43:21 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2100717269861167524
- type: original
- lang: en

```
the most underrated part of muse is the model that powers it. thank you to the MSL research team—some of the most technically brilliant, hardworking scientists and engineers i’ve ever known. you brought original ideas to difficult problems, invented new methods, and battled through enormous technical complexity to build the jewel at the heart of muse.

thank you to the teams working on agentic post-training, tool and computer use, memory, personalization, alignment, safety, and multimodal understanding. thank you to the people building data, evals, and infrastructure beneath it all. so much scientific ingenuity lives in those details: designing a better experiment, finding the flaw in an apparently good result, staying with a stubborn problem until a new way forward becomes clear.

and now that jewel powers a beautiful product your grandma can use without knowing a thing about the extraordinary complexity underneath. it will just work for her. all those discoveries, all that patient work, becoming something simple and useful in another person’s hands. there is something magical about that. i’m grateful to be building with you.
```

```
We believe strongly in the necessity to invest into alignment.

1. People and businesses will only use agents that are aligned with their intent and values. If we do not build models aligned with people and businesses, then they will move to more aligned options.

2. Every lab should have a strong governance framework across training and deployment. This should include external evaluators, which are best practice for transparency, and independent oversight on things like safety criteria for model launches.

3. Every lab will need to operate within institutional protections of democratic countries. This means labs face significant liability if their models cause harm. This will push the ecosystem in the right ways.

4. Advances in the field are ultimately downstream of compute and resource allocation. Racing on recursive self-improvement is one of the riskiest pathways for potential loss of control to powerful models. Meta is committing the significant majority of our compute towards serving people rather than racing on RSI, and other labs can choose to do the same.

AI is a very powerful technology, and there is immense responsibility in developing it safely alongside the right checks and balances.
```

## 2099337296487333978

- time: 2026-09-14 11:19:49 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2099337296487333978
- type: original
- lang: en

```
something people may have missed:

we have been building our models (muse spark 1 through 1.3) specifically to be exceptional for muse over many months.

in each of our releases, we made big gains on agentic and multimodal capability. each of these steps were secretly to set the stage for our personal agent, @Muse. (they’re named the same thing for a reason… we planned ahead!)

when we started MSL last year, the goal was always to develop personal superintelligence. that always meant giving AI superpowers to everyone in the world, and the personal agent is the perfect manifestation of that.

building great models takes time, and it takes an enormous amount of planning and foresight to develop unique capabilities into these models. it often requires months of careful research and work across infra and data to build excellent models.

it is very sweet to see the culmination of over a year of hard work come together into a product that people are loving. we are so glad that people are enjoying muse, and we can’t wait to keep improving it!
```

- time: 2026-09-13 22:56:02 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2099150117156888757
- type: quote
- lang: en

```
fun fact: the muse feed was my idea (one of the few things that was) after realizing agents with strong memory recommend content to you in a very different way than existing recommendation systems

they’re much closer to a good friend sending you things they think you might like based on what they knows about you, vs a slew of content related to what you just clicked.

it’s just the beginning here, but very excited for people to try their muse feeds and see what they do with it!
```

## 2085081836489965629

- time: 2026-08-06 03:13:43 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2085081836489965629
- type: reply
- lang: en

```
we co-trained the model and agent together. the result: higher first-attempt success on tool calls, cleaner plan execution, less time re-prompting. 1m token context window.

one cool thing: we pointed it at kernel optimization on nvidia hopper and let it run. 1,000+ tool calls over 24 hours. it kept finding substantial improvements well beyond the initial exploration phase.
```

## 2085081834699022575

- time: 2026-08-06 03:13:42 GMT+8
- url: https://x.com/alexandr_wang/status/2085081834699022575
- type: reply
- lang: en

```
the harness architecture: multiple specialized agents coordinate in parallel on the same task. they persist across your session, building context instead of starting cold each time. for complex work, sub-agents fan out into isolated worktrees so your working copy stays clean. https://t.co/EgjtlUs0HU
```

```
- self-refinement (model improves its own output within its chain of thought — emerged during RL, not by design)
- multi-reference composition (many images blended into one coherent generation)
- multi-turn editing (iterate without losing coherence or starting over).
```

```
We are always open to feedback and welcome any perspective on weaknesses you've noticed in the model from using it.

We are quite upfront that our model does not perform well on ARC AGI 2 for example, and published those results for the community to understand. That might reflect some areas of improvement of the model that we could focus on in the future.

In general, we have been pleasantly surprised by users' feedback on the models in areas like visual coding, writing style, and reasoning queries.
```
